# C1 residual U1: SQLite/FTS5 memory on the native save's write path (store schema v13)

**Serves:** exit criterion 9, DEL-04-05 phase C1, residual U1. **Standing:** bounded
diagnostic evidence (Agent 0: "GO as BOUNDED DIAGNOSTIC EVIDENCE, not qualification of a universal
coefficient"). This note is a **proposal input only**. U1 stays **open** until a proposal is reviewed
and adopted. U3 (builder/reader/validator working memory, the page cache in general) and U4 (no
App-wide cap) are separate and stay open. Nothing here adds a charge, coefficient, budget,
ceiling, margin, pressure rule, App-wide cap or SQLite setting to the App.

**Store schema v13 history (2026-11-05).** Everything below was measured at store schema v13,
where every save replaced the project's FTS5 search row. Store schema v14 (RT-U4, Agent 0's
decision (A)) retires that sidecar: migration v14 drops `local_project_fts`, and no save builds a
search text or writes a search row. The figures, fits and envelopes here are kept unedited as v13
history. They do not describe the v14 save path, and they are not to be re-derived or applied to it.
U1 is now narrowed to SQLite's own memory for the canonical upserts and COMMIT (DEL-04-05
`c1-native-custody-plan.md`, "Residuals"). It stays open and unmeasured at v14. At v14,
`stored_read_sqlite_u1.rs` measures that remaining path: `open_store`, `write_transaction`, the
project row's upsert, COMMIT. It has not been run. The harness that produced §7 is the file at the
measured head below. U3 and U4 are unchanged.

**Measured code:** `apps/desktop/src-tauri/src/stored_read_sqlite_u1.rs` at
`197126003657899e9b370b877690dbb98ae9be9d` (branch `claude/rt-perf-c1-sqlite-u1`, from
`claude/rt-perf-c1-custody` at `b35cf465391d08706ed65f22bbbef9e539a25073`). The production
functions it calls (`stored_read::open_store`, `stored_read::write_transaction`,
`native_persistence::replaced_search_bytes`, `upsert_project_texts`, `replace_project_search`,
`open_project_store`, `apply_store_migrations`, `finish_save`) are byte-identical at that base and
at `claude/rt-perf-c1-custody` after #136. The results in §7 are the generator's output pasted
unedited. §§1–6 are analysis.

## 1. What was measured

**Path.** `finish_save`'s SQLite statements in its order, inside `run_save`'s write transaction:
`stored_read::open_store` (the production opener with its migrations: WAL, `foreign_keys`, busy
timeout), `stored_read::write_transaction` (BEGIN IMMEDIATE), `replaced_search_bytes` (its
`octet_length` query), `crate::upsert_project_texts`, `crate::replace_project_search` (the FTS
`DELETE … WHERE project_id = ?1`, then the `INSERT`), the transaction's COMMIT, and the close.
`stage_save`'s own statements are not run, and neither is any other command.

**Row texts.** The record fixture is the analysis-run record slot (`analysis_run_json`). The model is
a small fixed JSON (91 bytes for project A), the other slots are `[]`/`null`, and the result slot is `null`:
the result text sits in the project row, which the ledger prices, and not in the search text. So
|S| = fixture + 105 bytes, built by the production `ProjectTexts::search_text`.

**Isolation.** `u1_drive_all` spawns one fresh child process of the test binary per case
(`--ignored --exact stored_read::sqlite_u1::u1_measure_case --test-threads=1`), strictly one after
another. A child does only its own case's SQLite work, then exits. The driver itself does no SQLite
work. Setup runs in the same child before the measured window: the store is created and migrated
by `open_store`, and each earlier save is a full production save on its own connection
(`open_store`, `write_transaction`, `write_project_texts`, commit, close).

**Counters.** `sqlite3_status64(SQLITE_STATUS_MEMORY_USED)` gives the current value and the
high-water. `SQLITE_STATUS_MALLOC_SIZE` gives the largest request, and `SQLITE_STATUS_MALLOC_COUNT`
the allocation count. At every boundary all of them are read, then their high-waters are reset to
current. A boundary is:
- the end of each harness call: `baseline` (before the open), `open_store`, `write_transaction`,
  `replaced_search_bytes`, `upsert_project_texts`, `replace_project_search`, `commit` (after
  `Transaction::commit` returned, which includes the WAL autocheckpoint hook that runs after the
  COMMIT statement), and `close` (after the connection dropped);
- inside those calls, the start and the end of each top-level statement. These come from
  `sqlite3_trace_v2` `SQLITE_TRACE_STMT` / `SQLITE_TRACE_PROFILE` for the statement pointer that
  started while no other was running. The end event fires inside `sqlite3_step` once the statement
  is done, so COMMIT's work, including FTS5's `xSync` flush and merge, falls inside `exec: COMMIT`.
  FTS5's nested statements do not move a boundary. The statements FTS5 runs while the `DELETE` is
  being prepared (`PRAGMA data_version`, `SELECT k, v FROM …_config`) show up as their own
  boundaries, and they are counted in the delete phase.

Phases: *upsert* = from `replaced_search_bytes` to `upsert_project_texts`; *FTS delete* = from there
to the end of the `DELETE`; *FTS insert* = from there to `replace_project_search`'s return;
*COMMIT* = from there to `commit`. Each phase's high-water is the largest interval high-water in it
(absolute bytes). The memory statistics are on (compiled default `SQLITE_DEFAULT_MEMSTATUS=1`;
nothing in the process sets `sqlite3_config`). Every process read a nonzero current value after the
open (§7 G1, G5b). Had one read zero, it would have been recorded as unavailable evidence.

**Derived U1 metric (§7 G4).** R0 = current when `replace_project_search` begins. The ledger term is
`finish_save`'s reservation "SQLite's copies of the project's search row": id + name + |S| + the
search-row record + |S′| (from `replaced_search_bytes`), held only while `replace_project_search`
runs. *Excess* = max(delete hw, insert hw) − R0 − ledger term. *Δcommit* = COMMIT hw − R0, since no
SQLite reservation is held over COMMIT. **U1 = max(excess, Δcommit)**: the SQLite bytes above the
level where the replace started that the per-command ledger does not price. Because the current
value never exceeds a later high-water and the close returns it to 0, U1 also covers everything
from COMMIT's return to the close.

**Inputs (invented data only; generator `fixture()`; lengths and SHA-256 in §7 G2).** Sizes 1, 4, 16
and 64 MiB. Families:
- *repeated*: one token repeated;
- *distinct*: distinct `t<letter><hex>` tokens;
- *short*: the many-distinct extreme, with every token distinct and as short as possible (bijective
  base-36 numerals);
- *unicode*: long mixed-case, diacritic, Greek, Cyrillic and CJK tokens in an 8 / 300 / 5 000 /
  40 000-character cycle, the longest above FTS5's 32 768-byte token cap;
- *single*: the whole text is one Unicode token;
- *json*: node objects with pseudo-random decimals, resembling the stored record JSON.

**Cases (110).**
- Fresh write: 6 families × 4 sizes.
- Replacement of an existing search row in both directions, same family, with disjoint variants:
  1→1, 4→4, 16→16, 64→64, 1→4, 4→16, 16→64, 1→64, and the reverse shrinks 4→1, 16→4, 64→16, 64→1.
  That is 6 × 12.
- 4 cross-family replacements.
- 8 multi-project cases (M1–M8), where other projects' rows are stored before or after A's, with up
  to four other rows of up to 64 MiB.
- 2 history cases (H1, H2): four earlier saves of A, so that COMMIT's automerge folds earlier
  segments, including their delete markers.

**Environment (§7 G1, verbatim from the probe process).** SQLite `3.46.0`, source id
`2024-05-23 13:25:27 96c92aba00c8375bc32fafcdf12429c58bd8aabfcadab6683e35bbb9cdebf19e`, the bundled
amalgamation from `libsqlite3-sys` 0.30.1, `SYSTEM_MALLOC`, `THREADSAFE=1`. The full compile
options are in G1. The production connection reads `page_size` 4096, `cache_size` −2000,
`cache_spill` 483, `journal_mode` wal, `synchronous` 2, `wal_autocheckpoint` 1000, `temp_store` 0
and `mmap_size` 0. The only `local_project_fts_config` row is `version 4`, so every FTS5 option
(hash size, automerge, crisismerge, page size) is at its default. rusqlite, verbatim:

## 2. Limits of the measurement

- `SQLITE_STATUS_MEMORY_USED` is process-global and counts only SQLite's own allocations at the
  allocator's block size (`SYSTEM_MALLOC`; on macOS the zone's `size`). It does not see:
  - lookaside slots, which are carved from one block counted at open;
  - the allocator's transient copy inside `realloc`;
  - fragmentation;
  - Rust-side memory such as the texts and the search text, which the ledger prices separately.
- No timing, RSS or footprint was measured. There was no native App and no exclusive window.
- The trace callback allocates nothing in SQLite. For a nested statement, SQLite itself formats a
  `-- <sql>` copy before the callback runs, at most 142 bytes here (G5b), which is negligible.
- The cases are a bounded set: six families, four sizes, eight multi-project and two history
  layouts. One SQLite build (3.46.0, the options above), one platform (macOS, arm64) and one
  connection configuration were measured. Windows (RT-V7: a different allocator, with SQLite's
  8-byte size header in place of `malloc_size`) is not measured.
- Each command opens its own connection, as production does. Statement caches, FTS5's hash slot
  array and the tokenizer's fold buffer all start empty. A long-lived connection would keep the
  last two at their high size (see §5).

## 3. Results

All 110 cases were measured with memory statistics on. The baseline current was 0 in every process,
so SQLite holds nothing with no connection open, and the live bytes after the close were 0 in every
process (G5b). The full tables are §7 G3 and G4. In summary (bytes; U1 as defined in §1):

| case | \|S\| | \|S′\| | U1 | U1/\|S\| | set by |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|
| fresh/repeated/64 | 67108969 | 0 | 83958240 | 1.251 | COMMIT |
| fresh/unicode/64 | 67108969 | 0 | 110991488 | 1.654 | COMMIT |
| fresh/single/64 | 67108969 | 0 | 167838742 | 2.501 | replace |
| fresh/json/1 | 1048681 | 0 | 10050742 | 9.584 | replace |
| fresh/json/64 | 67108969 | 0 | 308418688 | 4.596 | COMMIT |
| fresh/distinct/64 | 67108969 | 0 | 1171735136 | 17.460 | COMMIT |
| fresh/short/1 | 1048681 | 0 | 33594166 | 32.035 | replace |
| fresh/short/64 | 67108969 | 0 | 1776741312 | 26.475 | COMMIT |
| replace/short/64->64 | 67108969 | 67108969 | 1776756288 | 26.476 | COMMIT |
| replace/short/64->1 | 1048681 | 67108969 | 1707646365 | 1628.375 | replace (delete) |
| replace/repeated/64->1 | 1048681 | 67108969 | 1401360 | 1.336 | COMMIT |
| multi/M1 (B json16, A fresh json1) | 1048681 | 0 | 15884310 | 15.147 | replace (delete scan) |

Findings:

1. **FTS5's pending hash for S dominates, and its cost depends on the text's tokens, not its
   length.** For fresh writes from 64 MiB down to 1 MiB, U1/|S| was 1.25–2.39 for one repeated
   token, 1.65–3.07 for long Unicode tokens, 2.50–3.64 for a single token, 4.60–9.58 for
   record-like JSON, 17.5–24.1 for 8-character distinct tokens, and 26.5–32.0 for the shortest
   distinct tokens (G4). The ratio is higher at 1 MiB because of the fixed overhead. The hash is
   built during the FTS `INSERT` and stays live until COMMIT flushes it, so U1 is set by Δcommit in
   72 cases and by the replace's excess in 38 (G5b). COMMIT adds at most
   203 040 bytes over what it started with (G5b): the flush streams.
2. **The replaced row costs as much as a new one when it is larger.** The DELETE loads S′ and adds
   one delete marker per distinct token of S′. A large S′ is flushed at the INSERT's start, but the
   hash's slot array stays at its grown size. So a shrink is set by S′ (short 64→1: 1.71 GB for a
   1 MiB new text), and an equal-size replacement costs about the same as a fresh write of the
   larger text (short 64→64 = fresh/short/64).
3. **Unrelated projects' rows count.** The DELETE's `project_id = ?` is a full scan. During the
   delete phase, the largest other stored search text is resident, one row at a time: 1.004–1.072
   × Tmax in M1–M4 and M7, and 1.277 × Tmax in M8, whose 4 MiB rows make the fixed overhead
   visible. With three 16 MiB others (M7) the rise was still 1.072 × Tmax, not the sum (G5b). In
   M5, the 2.0 × Tmax rise is A's own json-4 MiB S′ and its delete markers, not two rows. In M6
   the delete markers dominate.
4. **Bytes kept after COMMIT until the connection closes** are consistent with |S| + the hash's
   slot array + the tokenizer's fold buffer:
   - fresh/repeated/64: 67.15 MB ≈ |S|;
   - fresh/single/64: 134.26 MB ≈ |S| + a 2²⁶-byte fold buffer;
   - fresh/short/64: 335.57 MB ≈ |S| + a 268 435 456-byte slot array, which is also that case's
     largest single allocation;
   - fresh/json/16: 33.58 MB ≈ |S| + 16.8 MB, the size of a 2²¹-slot array.

   This is consistent with the source (§5). The distinct-token counts were not measured, so the
   slot-array sizes are inferred.
5. **Page cache.** 2 103 040 bytes were in use after every COMMIT (G5b), which is cache_size −2000
   plus page headers. This is observed after-COMMIT occupancy, not a cap; `cache_size` is a
   suggested maximum (§5). History merges (H1, H2: all segments merged into one at COMMIT) showed no
   added peak.
6. **Upsert phase (outside U1's metric, recorded for U1/U3).** The upsert's rise over its start
   exceeds 2·|S| by about 2.5 MB in most cases. In six equal-size replacements (json 4→4 and
   16→16, distinct 16→16 and 64→64, cross json16→distinct16, cross distinct64→repeated64) it
   exceeds it by |S| + 2.5 MB, up to 69 726 046 bytes, and that is released by the statement's end.
   It does not appear in other equal-size cases (json 64→64, M6, H1, the repeated, unicode and
   single families). Its size matches a page-cache excursion holding the whole overflow chain
   (16 384 pages × ~4.25 KB), but the mechanism has **not** been identified. The ledger's upsert
   reservation (bound texts + the new row's record) does not cover it.

## 4. Empirical candidate envelope (measured cases only)

Over the 110 measured cases (§7 G5, G5b), with Tmax the largest other stored search text:

- the least c with U1 ≤ c·max(|S|, |S′|) + 4 MiB is **28.045**, and with k = 0 it is 32.045. A base
  of |S| alone does not work: shrinks need 1 628 × |S|.
- candidate **E = 36·max(|S|, |S′|) + 1.25·Tmax + 4 MiB** has a least E/U1 of **1.248**, so it
  covers every measured case with at least a 24.8 % margin. The case closest to it is
  replace/short/1→1. The 1.25·Tmax term is set by the multi-project delete scans (1.004–1.072 ×
  Tmax measured). The 4 MiB constant covers the 2 MiB page cache, the 1 MiB pending-flush threshold
  and statement overhead.

**This envelope is not an upper bound.** It was fitted after the data to these 110 inputs. It does
not establish a bound for every text even at 1–64 MiB, nor for any size outside that range, nor for
another SQLite build, platform or connection setting. Texts of shorter distinct tokens than the
`short` family were not constructed. That includes 2-byte-UTF-8 letters as one- and two-character
tokens, and mixed ASCII/non-ASCII tokens. They would cost more per byte (§5).

## 5. Source-backed account (SQLite 3.46.0 as linked, FTS5 defaults, unicode61)

References are to functions in the bundled `sqlite3.c`.

- **Hash entry** (`sqlite3Fts5HashWrite`). A new token allocates `sizeof(Fts5HashEntry)` (48 bytes,
  by its layout on a 64-bit target) + token + 2 + 64 bytes, at least 128. So every distinct token of up to 14 bytes costs
  128 bytes. When fewer than 22 bytes remain, the entry doubles (`sqlite3_realloc64`). A position
  appends a varint of (delta + 2), 1–5 bytes. A delete marker (`iCol < 0`, from
  `fts5StorageDeleteFromIndex`) sets `bDel` and appends nothing per occurrence.
- **Slot array** (`fts5HashResize`). The array has 8 bytes per slot, starts at 1 024 slots, and
  doubles when `nEntry*2 >= nSlot`. So nSlot ≤ 4·nEntry: up to 32 bytes per entry, and 48 bytes
  while the old and new arrays coexist. `sqlite3Fts5HashClear` frees the entries but keeps the
  array. It is freed only with the index, so it lives as long as the connection.
- **Flush rules** (`sqlite3Fts5IndexBeginWrite`). The hash is flushed only when a row write begins,
  if rowids go backwards, or if the same rowid is inserted again after an insert, or if
  `nPendingData > nHashSize` (1 MiB default, `FTS5_DEFAULT_HASHSIZE`). There is no flush inside a
  row. So one row's entire index (hash(S)) is resident from tokenization until COMMIT's
  `sqlite3Fts5IndexSync` → `fts5IndexFlush`. S′'s delete markers are flushed at the INSERT's start
  if they exceed 1 MiB of pending data; otherwise they coexist with hash(S).
- **COMMIT** (`fts5FlushOneHash`). The hash is sorted in place through `pScanNext` and written leaf
  by leaf through `Fts5Buffer`s of page size 4 050. Automerge (4) and crisismerge (16) merge page
  by page. `cache_size` is SQLite's suggested maximum for the page cache, not a hard cap:
  `pcache1FetchStage2` can allocate beyond it when the pages it needs cannot be recycled, and
  `cache_spill` writes dirty pages to the WAL without capping the cache (review of #137, finding 2).
  The ≈2 MiB in §3 is the occupancy observed after COMMIT under these settings, not an
  in-statement maximum; transient cache growth within a statement is unresolved.
- **Copies of S.**
  - The rusqlite bind copies S (`SQLITE_TRANSIENT`), and the ledger prices it as the search text.
  - FTS5's `sqlite3Fts5StorageContentInsert` binds S again with `sqlite3_bind_value` into its cached
    `INSERT_CONTENT` statement, then steps and resets. It never clears the bindings, so this copy
    lives until the connection closes. It is unpriced.
  - The content record is priced as the search row.
  - The measured insert peak, 3|S| + hash in fresh/repeated/64, also contains one more |S|-sized
    value during the statement; which statement holds it was not traced to a line.
- **Tokenizer** (`fts5UnicodeTokenize`). unicode61 folds each whole token into `aFold`, doubling
  the buffer as needed. The old and new buffers coexist during the copy, which is the +0.5|S| in
  fresh/single/64. FTS5 caps a token at 32 768 bytes (`FTS5_MAX_TOKEN_SIZE`) only afterwards, in
  `fts5StorageInsertCallback`. `aFold` is freed only in `fts5UnicodeDelete`, at the connection's
  close. So a text's longest token costs up to about 2× its folded length, kept for the
  connection's life.
- **DELETE plan** (`fts5BestIndexMethod`). FTS5 consumes `=` only on rowid or the hidden table
  column. `project_id = ?` (any ordinary column, UNINDEXED or not) is left to SQLite, which scans
  every content row through `FTS5_STMT_SCAN` (all content columns). That puts the largest stored
  row in memory once, during the scan. `fts5StorageDeleteFromIndex` then loads S′ through the
  `LOOKUP` statement and tokenizes it into delete markers.

**What a production charge would have to cover**, held from the start of the search replace until
the connection closes:

(a) FTS5's own bound copy of S: |S|, held until the close.

(b) hash(S): 128 bytes per distinct token of up to 14 bytes (more for longer ones), plus up to
twice the position bytes, which are at most 5 bytes per occurrence.

(c) The slot array: up to 48 bytes per distinct token while it grows, sized to the larger of S's and
S′'s distinct counts, held until the close.

(d) The fold buffer: up to about 2× S's longest token, plus a 1.5× growth transient, held until
the close.

(e) The larger of:
- the delete phase: S′ + its delete markers (128 bytes per distinct token of S′) + its slot array;
- the scan: the largest other stored row;
- the content insert's |S|-sized values.

(f) The COMMIT flush's buffers, at most about 0.2 MB measured, plus the WAL autocheckpoint.

(g) The page cache: about 2 MiB observed after every COMMIT under these settings. That is
occupancy, not a cap (`cache_size` is a suggested maximum), and growth within a statement is
unresolved. The general page cache belongs to U3, and the upsert excursion in §3 item 6 is
unexplained.

**A sizing estimate per byte (incomplete; not a ceiling).** Each distinct token takes at least 2
bytes of text (1 token byte + 1 separator), so pricing only **minimum-sized** distinct-token
entries and their slots gives (128 + 48) / 2 = **88 bytes per byte of S** (likewise of S′ for
markers), plus 8 KiB, plus (a), (d) and the |S|-sized copies. This does **not** establish an upper
bound (review of #137, finding 1). It does not cover the larger allocations of longer tokens, the
growth of a repeated token's position list, allocator size rounding, or a final token without a
separator; no allocation-size inequality covering those terms is derived here. Counting how many short distinct tokens exist lowers the achievable
figure: there are only 36 one-character ASCII tokens, and every 4-character token takes 5 bytes. A
rough, unverified estimate for MiB-scale texts is 35–44×, against 26.5–32× measured for the
`short` family. That counting depends on unicode61's token-character classes (2-byte UTF-8 letters
as one- and two-character tokens) and diacritic folding, and it is **not proven here**.

**What remains unproven or open:**
- a counting bound for hash(S) under unicode61's character classes;
- allocator-internal `realloc` copying, which the counter cannot see. (Slot-array resizes are
  different: `fts5HashResize` allocates the new array with `sqlite3_malloc64` while the old one
  is still live, then frees it, so that overlap **is** in the high-water.)
- an allocation-size bound for hash(S) covering longer tokens, position-list growth, rounding and
  the final token (§5's 88 is an incomplete estimate);
- transient page-cache growth within a statement (only after-COMMIT occupancy was observed);
- which statement holds the extra |S| at the insert peak;
- the upsert's page-cache-sized excursion;
- other SQLite versions, compile options, platforms (Windows) and connection lifetimes. A
  long-lived connection would keep (a), (c) and (d) between commands;
- `stage_save`'s statements before `finish_save`, which were not run here;
- concurrency between commands. U4 (no App-wide cap) applies.

## 6. Matters for the proposal and review

- The ledger's search reservation (|S| + record + |S′|) covers about two of the measured
  components. A U1 charge would have to cover (a)–(e) above, held until the connection closes,
  where the current reservation ends when `replace_project_search` returns.
- The charge cannot be a function of |S| alone. It needs a term for S′ (shrinks) and one for the
  largest other stored search row. The last needs that row's length from the store before the
  write: the full scan comes from the plan, not from the data.
- A per-byte coefficient is either empirical (§4: 36 with the stated non-proof) or would need a
  source-derived bound, which this note does not establish (§5's 88 is an incomplete estimate for
  minimum-sized tokens only, not a ceiling). The choice, and whether to change the plan instead
  (for example an indexed `project_id` mapping, or a contentless/external-content table), are
  reserved for the proposal's review. Nothing here changes product behaviour.
- U3 (working memory, the page cache in general, the upsert excursion) and U4 (no App-wide cap)
  remain independent and explicit.

#### G4. Per case: rise over the replace's start and the ledger's search charge (bytes)

R0 = current when `replace_project_search` began. Δrep = max(FTS delete hw, FTS insert hw) − R0. Ledger = `finish_save`'s "SQLite's copies of the project's search row" reservation (id + name + |S| + search-row record + |S'|), held only while `replace_project_search` runs. Excess = Δrep − ledger. Δcommit = COMMIT hw − R0 (the ledger holds no SQLite reservation for COMMIT). U1 = max(excess, Δcommit). Largest = the largest single SQLite allocation request in the window.
